2. Curriculum Overview English
===============================

(upbeat music) -: Welcome back everyone, the Course Curriculum Lecture. Before we begin the course, let's build an understanding of our overall learning path. This lecture is just going to be a brief overview of the main topics and the structure of the curriculum of the course. That way you can understand the way the course is laid out and maybe you can jump to a particular section that interests you or understand the pathway from start to finish. So the overall course sections are Python text basics, NLP Python basics, part of speech tagging, text classification, semantics and sentiment analysis, topic modeling, and then some chatbots and advanced material that fall under the deep learning category.

We're gonna start off with Python text basics where we're just gonna learn the very basics of Python's built-in text capabilities. Those are things like learning how to open text files, how to work with PDF files, and the basics of regular expressions. Then we'll move on to NLP Python basics. This is when we actually start using specialized natural language processing libraries such as spaCy and NLTK. These are the two most popular NLP libraries for Python and we'll have a deeper discussion on the differences between them and why you may want to use another like spaCy in certain situations or NLTK in other situations.

So that's where we really begin to work with natural language processing libraries. Then we're going to move on and within that section we'll discuss things like spaCy pipelines, tokenization, stemming, lemmatization, stop words, and vocabulary and phrase matching. The next section is essentially going to be a continuation of learning how to work with those libraries by talking about things like part of speech tagging, named entity recognition, sentence segmentation of spaCy, and then we'll also see how to visualize parts of speech and named entity recognition.

Then we'll switch gears and talk about text classification. This is where we dive deeper into machine learning. learning about how to use Scikit-learn for machine learning, just an overall process and then we'll talk about text feature extraction, conducting operations like count vectorization and then TF-IDF vectorization, term frequency inverse document frequency, and then we'll have a text classification project for you. After that, we'll talk about semantics and sentiment analysis, semantics and things like word vectors such as the word2vec algorithm and then sentiment analysis, understanding whether a piece of text is positive or negative sentiment.

Then we move on to topic modeling. This is again on the machine learning side of things but we'll be switching gears to unsupervised learning. We'll talk about things like Latent Dirichlet allocation and non-negative matrix factorization. And this at the end we'll have a topic modeling project with LDA and NMF. Towards the end we're gonna have a general deep learning section where we talk about things like chatbots and advanced material. So here we're gonna focus on more complex and more advanced topics, like actually generating text using recurrent neural networks and long short term memory units.

And then we'll also talk about using deep learning to create your own chatbots. Okay, let's get started. I'll see you at the next lecture.

[calm music] -: Welcome, everyone, to this lecture on setting up spaCy. Before we set up spaCy and download the language library, let's quickly discuss what spaCy actually is. spaCy is an open source natural language processing library for Python. It's designed to effectively handle NLP tasks with the most efficient implementation of common tasks and algorithms. That means, for many natural language processing tasks, spaCy only has one implemented method: choosing the most efficient algorithm currently available.

Usually, that means state-of-the-art. This means you often don't actually have the option to choose between algorithms for each particular task. spaCy only has one option, which is the most efficient currently available. Now, NLTK is another library we're going to be using throughout this course, and this one's more common, so you may have already heard of it. NLTK stands for the Natural Language Toolkit, and it's a very popular open source library. It was initially released in 2001, so that means it's much older than spaCy, which was released in 2015.

It also provides many functionalities, but includes less efficient implementations, and that's probably one of the main differences between spaCy and NLTK. So, let's quickly compare the two. For many common NLP tasks, spaCy is much faster and more efficient, at the cost of the user not being able to choose a specific algorithmic implementation. For most use cases, that really doesn't matter, because you care about the end result, not using a particular form of the algorithm.

So, for that reason, spaCy is much faster than NLTK, because NLTK has a variety of implementations for a lot of common tasks, when spaCy just defaults to the most efficient currently available. And, it's also much newer. Keep in mind, spaCy doesn't include some pre-created models for some applications that NLTK does, such as sentiment analysis, which is typically easier to perform with NLTK. That means, in this course, due to spaCy's state-of-the-art approach and efficiency, we're gonna focus on using spaCy where it really matters.

15. What is Natural Language Processing English
================================================

(soft music) -: Welcome back, everyone. So far, we've learned how to work with text files with Python, do some things like regular expressions, as well as work with PDF files. But I wouldn't say that those processes that we've learned are really natural language processing. So what is NLP, or natural language processing? For a formal definition, according to Wikipedia, natural language processing, or NLP for short, is an area of computer science and artificial intelligence concerned of the interactions between computers and human that is natural languages, in particular, how to program computers to process and analyze large amounts of natural language data.

Now, to put this in more simple terms, often when performing some sort of analysis, usually have lots of data that's numerical, and that's really convenient. Things like sales numbers, physical measurements, quantifiable categories. And computers are already very good at handling direct numerical information. However, what do we do with computers about actual natural language data, that is, text data? As humans, it's really easy for us to tell that there is a plethora of information inside of a text document, like a PDF file, an email, a book.

There's lots of information that we can easily access, because we're humans and we understand how to read that natural language. But a computer needs specialized processing techniques in order to understand raw text data. Text data is highly unstructured, and can also be in multiple languages, not just English. However, we'll only be dealing with English language in this course. Natural language processing attempts to use a variety of techniques in order to create some sort of structure out of raw text data.

In this section of the course, we're going to start actually discussing some of these basic techniques, which is why we didn't really introduce this lecture of what is NLP until now. So these techniques are built into the library, such as spaCy and NLTK. Some example use cases of natural language processing is taking a raw text email and classifying it as spam versus a legitimate email, or taking a raw text movie review and actually grabbing the sentiment analysis, being able to tell if the review was positive or negative.

And these are things we're actually going to do during the course. And we can also do things like analyze trends from written customer feedback forms, or even understand text commands, such as when you're talking to your phone and you say something like, "Hey, Google, play this song," or "Hey, Siri," or "Hey, Alexa." That needs natural language processing in order to convert that text information to something the computer can understand. So natural language processing is constantly evolving, and great strides are made even every month.

Here we're going to focus on fundamental ideas that all state-of-the-art techniques are based off of. So let's begin by learning about the basics of using the spaCy library. Coming up next, we'll talk about spaCy basics. I'll see you there.

17. Tokenization Part One English

(gentle music) -: Welcome to this lecture on tokenization. Tokenization is the process of breaking up the original raw text into component pieces otherwise known as tokens. This was the very first step that occurred when we created our document objects using the pipeline with spaCy. You can imagine that we have some original text and the very first step is to split on the white space. So from "We're moving to LA", we split on the white space. Then spaCy is smart enough to understand that it's going to separate prefix that is characters at the beginning.

It's also going to separate exceptions. Those are concatenations. And it's also going to be able to separate a suffix. So those are characters towards the end. And then we can also realize that we have exceptions such as an exclamation point, and then we're done. So there's lots of steps involved but luckily spaCy's taken care of all of that for us. We have prefix, suffix, infix and exceptions. So again, notice that the tokens are just pieces of the original text.

We don't see any conversion to words stems or lemmas, the base forms of words. And we haven't seen anything yet about organizations, places, money, et cetera. Everything that helps us understand the meaning of the text is derived from tokens and the relationship to one another. Now, as I mentioned, we had the prefix, those were the characters at the beginning.

We had the suffix, characters at the end. We have infix, which are the characters in between the prefix and the suffix. And then we have the exception which is a special case rule to split a string into several tokens or prevent a token from being split when punctuation rules are applied. So we can see a few examples here such as a prefix is a dollar sign prefixing an amount of money. A suffix can be something like "km" suffix at the end, the kilometers traveled.

19. Stemming English
=====================

-: Welcome everyone to this lecture on stemming. Often when searching texts for a certain keyword, it helps if the search returns variations of the word. For instance, searching for the term 'boat' might also return things like 'boats' and 'boating'. Here the stem 'boat' is going to be the stem for words like 'boat', 'boater', 'boating', and 'boats', which all are conveying the idea related to boat. Stemming is a somewhat crude method for cataloging related words.

It essentially just chops off letters from the end until the stem is actually reached. This works fairly well in most cases, but unfortunately, English has so many exceptions where a more sophisticated process is usually required. In fact, it's such a rough process that spaCy doesn't even include a stemmer. Instead, they opt for a more sophisticated method known as lemmatization which we're gonna cover in the next lecture. But you'll often see stemming in natural language processing discussion.

So it's important that you do have an understanding of the basics of stemming before you move on to lemmatization. There is a link in the notebook to a discussion on why the maintainers of spaCy actually decide not to include a stemmer in favor of lemmatization in case you're interested in the history of the development of spaCy. So you can check out the notebook for that link discussion. Now, because of this decision not to include stemming in spaCy we're now going to jump over to using NLTK and learn about various stemmers.

In fact, we're gonna show you both the Porter Stemmer and the Snowball Stemmer. So one of the most common and effective stemming tools is Porter's algorithm developed by Martin Porter in 1980. And the algorithm employs five phases of word reduction each with its own set of mapping rules. So in the first phase simple suffix mapping rules are defined such as the following: So here we have a table with S1 going to S2, essentially going from one word to a stem.

So you can see here that the ending of one SSES will get reduced to SS or caresses gets reduced to caress or IES gets reduced to I. So ponies gets reduced to p o n i and ties gets reduced to t I. So from a given set of stemming rules only one rule is applied, based off the longest suffix, S1. Thus caresses reduces to caress but not to cares. That way there's no confusion and mixing up different types of words to the wrong stem. Now, there are some more sophisticated phases that consider the length and complexity of the word before applying a rule such as trying to map relational or national to their stem relate and nation, or agreed and feed to their other stem.

Agree and feed. Notice that we're going from e e d to e e or ational to a t e. So there's lots of different complex rules that the Porter Stemmer is going to implement. Now Snowball is the name of a stemming language that was also developed by Martin Porter. And the algorithm here is actually sometimes called the English Stemmer or Porter Two Stemmer, and it offers a slight improvement over the original Porter Stemmer, both in logic and speed. So you can essentially think of it as an improvement over the original Porter Stemmer.

So let's use Python and NLTK to actually show how to use these stemmers with Python. We'll jump over to a notebook now. All right, the first thing I'm going to do is import NLTK for the natural language toolkit. And then from NLTK dot stem dot porter, I'm going to import the porter Stemmer. And then I'm going to create an instance of this Porter Stemmer object. And I'm just using tab to auto complete there. And let's create a list of words. So I'm gonna have 'run' and 'runner' and then I'm going to have also 'ran'.

So notice technically these are all conveying the same idea. They just have different tenses to them. So run, runner, and ran. Actually one is a noun versus these are verbs but you can see they're both conveying, or all three of 'em are conveying, the same idea of running. And we're gonna add one more 'runs' and let's add in a few more words like 'easily'. And we'll also put in 'fairly' just so we can get some more examples. And the way you stem down these words is you just pass 'em into the stem method of this Porter Stemmer.

So the way this works is we can say 'for word' in words we can print out the original word and then I'm going to concatenate an arrow to show you what it stems to. And then off our stemmer object, so off p stemmer, you call stem and then pass in the particular word. So all I'm doing here is I'm gonna print out the original word from this list. Then I'm gonna print out an arrow with a string and then I'm going to show the stemmed version of the word using the Porter Stemmer.

So when I run this I can see that 'run' goes to 'run', 'runner' goes to 'runner', 'ran' goes to 'ran'. 'Runs', however does get stemmed down to 'run'. So notice, according to the Porter Stemmer rules we should chop off the S and now run and runs get treated the same. And what's interesting here is that easily and fairly they get stemmed down to ending with an I, which is kind of interesting here. So the stemmer is actually able to recognize that 'runner' is a noun, not a verb form or a adverb.

And these adverbs right here, 'easily' and 'fairly' they're stemmed to the unusual root of easily with an i and fairly with an i as well. So keep that in mind if you're using the Porter Stemmer. As we mentioned, there's a more sophisticated and slightly better version of the stemmer called the Snowball Stemmer. And in order to use it you just need to say from NLTK dot stem dot snowball Import and call the snowball stemmer. And then you can create an instance of that stemmer by saying Snowball Stemmer.

And this one also requires you to tell it what language it's in. So we're just gonna pass in 'English' as the language parameter and we're gonna use the same words as before. So now we're gonna say 'for word' in words, print out the word and then we'll say print out in concatenation what the Snowball Stemmer stems it to. So s stemer, stem, and then word. And when you run this you notice that the stemming performs slightly differently 'run' and 'runs', both get stemmed down to 'run' but now 'fairly' just goes down to 'fair', which is interesting cause maybe that performs better for things like 'fairness'.

20. Lemmatization English
==========================

(bright ethereal music) -: Welcome back, everyone, to this lecture on lemmatization. In contrast to stemming, lemmatization looks beyond word reduction and considers a language's full vocabulary to apply a morphological analysis towards. The lemma of "was" is "be", and the lemma of "mice" is "mouse". So we're not just shortening words or cutting off the end of them. Instead, we're looking at the full context of the word. Furthermore, the lemma of "meeting" might be "meet" or "meeting", depending on its actual use in a sentence.

Lemmatization is typically seen as much more informative than simple stemming, which is why the spaCy library has opted to only have lemmatization available instead of simple stemming. And again, lemmatization is going to look at the surrounding text to determine a given word's part of speech. It doesn't categorize words into just general phrases. In an upcoming lecture, we're gonna investigate word vectors and similarity in a lot more detail. But for right now, let's learn how to perform lemmatization with spaCy.

Let's jump over to a Jupyter Notebook. Okay, to begin, we need to do our standard imports for spaCy, which is importing spaCy and then also loading in the actual language library to use. In our case, it's going to be spacy.load and then "en_core_web_sm". So run that. Again, that may take some time, depending on your computer. And we're gonna create a Doc object, going to pass in a Unicode string here, and say, "I am a runner, running in a race." And let's say, "Because I love to run since I ran today." Okay, so lots of different use cases for words that seem to all have, at their core of the idea, running.

So runner, running, race, run, and ran. But there are some distinct differences. Things like, a runner is a noun versus running is a verb. A race itself is actually a noun. And then run, in this case, here, that's a verb. And ran is a past tense verb. Let's go ahead and create that document. And then what we're going to do is say for token in our document, we're going to print out the following: We'll print out the token text. And then we're gonna have a tab.

So, \t introduces a tab. And then we're going to say print out the token part of speech. So, pos_. Then we'll print out another tab. Then we'll say print out the lemma of the tokens. So that's the actual lemmatization. And notice here, when we say .lemma, that's actually gonna input, or output a number. So we'll talk about that in just a little bit. And then we'll put in another tab. This is essentially just for formatting. And then we'll say token.lemma_. So notice here, one of these is just lemma, the other one is lemma_.

Go ahead and run this. And you should see, more or less, a table that kinda looks like this. The formatting may be slightly off, depending on these numbers, if one of them is larger or if this word is a little longer than the shorter words. But overall, you should see the original word as the token "I am a runner", the part of speech, pronoun, verb, noun, adverb, et cetera. And then here, we actually have a number. So that number is going to point to a specific lemma inside of this language library.

Remember, this language library supported somewhere around like more than 50,000 words. So each of those has actually an individual hash to its lemma that we can then reference. And then the lemma_, that's the actual lemma that we're going to be referring to here. Now, something that's interesting to note here is that some of these words are actually getting reduced down to the same lemma, even though they're being used in different tenses. So running, run, and ran are all getting reduced down to the lemma "run".

And you can actually check that it's the same lemma "run" by taking a look at the actual hash value for this particular lemma. So each lemma has its own individual hash reference. And we're gonna be seeing that sort of behavior a lot with spaCy, in that not only can you access the lemmas or the parts of speech with these underscore arguments at the very end of this attribute, but there's also a hash code that then you can use essentially as a lookup for this English language, which is almost asking, or it's acting like almost like a huge hash table with a bunch of lemmas and parts of speech that you can look up.

Okay. Now, you'll notice that this sort of printing out is a little sloppy because of the way that some of these tokens may be longer than others. So you get these sort of weird outputs. So we've already seen a little bit of parts of speech in previous lectures, but let's go and take a deeper dive into POS or parts of speech and what that really means and how we can use spaCy to actually grab parts of speech tags. It's important to understand that most words themselves are actually rare in occurrence inside of the context of an entire document. And it's common for words that look completely different to mean almost the same thing.

And the same words in a different order can actually then end up meaning something completely different. So really there's a lot of nuance to the way that words are being used and that is why we need to also look at the part of speech that word is being used in, not just the word itself. So when you're doing things like splitting text into useful word-like units like tokenization, that itself can be difficult in many languages. And while it's possible to solve some problems starting from only the raw characters or those raw tokens, it's usually better to use linguistic knowledge to add useful information to know if you mean a noun versus a verb versus an adjective.

That part of speech information is really crucial to taking your natural language processing to the next level. So that's exactly what spaCy is designed to do. You put in the raw text and you get back this document object that comes with a variety of annotations. In this lecture, we're going to be taking a closer look at the actual part of speech tags that spaCy gives us. And it gives us two types. It gives us a coarse part of speech tags. Those are your basic part of speech.

Things like nouns, verbs, and adjectives. But it also gives you fine-grained tags, more nuanced information like whether something's a plural noun or a past tense verb or a superlative adjective. We can say for key value in and we can call sorted pos counts items which is essentially a list of tuples we can print out. Let's use a little f-string formatting here.

We'll print out the key and then do a little space. We'll say doc vocab at that particular key, print out the text and then I'm gonna add in a little bit of formatting here that f-string literal formatting. And then we can pass in V, the actual value for it. So let's make sure we didn't commit a typo here. Run this and there we go. So this telling us that for numerical code 83 which is an adjective, we have three adjectives. We have three nouns, one punctuation, and so on.

And keep in mind you can do this not just for the coarse part of speech tags but for the fine-grained tags as well. Recall again from our table that right now we're just looking at this part of speech, this more coarse tag but there's also these fine-grained tags and we could do the same thing for it. So we can come back here and essentially copy and paste this. But then what we're gonna do is instead of saying POS counts, we're gonna say tag counts. And let's create that tag counts object.

We'll say tag counts is equal to the documents and we'll say count_by and then we'll say spacy.attrs for attributes. And instead of saying pos, we'll say tag. And then if you run that, now you get to see the tags. So again, notice here we have the numerical value the fine-grained part speech and then how many times it showed up. Now you're probably wondering why are these numbers so much larger than the numbers for the basic tags like adjective and noun?

Well, the reason for that is because in spaCy certain text values are hard coded into the document vocab, So that's these doc.vocab and they take up the first several hundred ID numbers. So the most common ones are the ones that are closer up in the list. So things like nouns and punctuations those show up really commonly. So that's why they're kind of smaller numbers. They're further up in that list closer to that first item in the list. The ones that don't get used that often they get pushed way further down.

I remember doc vocab also has essentially all the English words available to it. Remember the length of that vocab. If we check it out it's actually this really big vocabulary of 57,863. So for purpose of efficiency the most common ones are placed closer to the front of the list. That way when you search for them, it's more efficient. And the ones that are used less those get put down with kind of larger hashes. And that really has to do more with the way that spaCy works internally through its own internal operations than things you're gonna see as a user on your end.

So as a user on our end, we're working with spaCy. The size of this number is kind of irrelevant to us. We just care that there is a number identifier for each particular text code of either a part of speech tag or a particular fine-grained tag. And we can also do the exact same thing for syntactic dependencies. So again, we can copy and paste this code here and we can say DEP counts for syntactic dependencies, look up dep. So basically changing tag everywhere to dep run that.

And you can see these syntactic dependency tags. Again, kind of less common ones having larger numbers here and so on. Okay, so that's all there is for our discussion right now. Coarse POS tags versus fine-grained POS tags. The main things to understand from this lecture is that you can always call the tags off any token by saying POS underscore and tag underscore. If you forget the underscore it's just going to show you that raw number. For example, if you have a data point as a segment, a text, it could have a categorical label, such as, the text of an email being labeled as spam versus legitimate or the text of a movie review being labeled as a positive movie review or a negative movie review. The learning algorithm or machine learning algorithm is going to receive a set of inputs along with the corresponding correct outputs and the algorithm is going to learn by comparing its actual output with the correct outputs in order to find an error and then it's going to modify the internal parameters of the machine learning algorithm accordingly to achieve the best results on its own, without you explicitly programming it what it should change.

Supervised learning is commonly used in applications where historical data points predict likely future events such as historical information of previous emails you've received that someone has labeled as legitimate or illegitimate or spam. That historical data can be used in order to provide a future spam filter for future emails. So let's talk about this supervised machine learning process. And this is probably the most fundamental thing to get out of this particular lecture.

So if you're trying to build an email spam filter, right now, it's working on that training set of data. And the training set of data is commonly about 70% of the total data. It doesn't matter if you're trying to predict categories with eight different types of classes, or eight different types of categories. Your model fundamentally only has two outputs, either a correct output or an incorrect output when it comes to classification.

Now, for the purposes of explaining the metrics, we're gonna imagine a binary classification situation, where we only have two available classes, spam versus ham. In our example, we're going to be attempting to predict if a text message is spam or ham. Ham being another word for a legitimate message. Then what we do, is we simply compare the correct label that we already know to be true against the prediction that the train model output.

So here we say, was ham equal to ham? And in this case it's correct. So here we get a correct prediction. Keep in mind that for other data points the train model could be incorrect. It might say, "Oh, I think this text message is spam." And in that case, it would be an incorrect prediction. So what we're going to do is we just repeat this process for all the text messages inside of our test data. At the end, we're gonna have a count of correct matches and a count of incorrect matches.

Here's the absolute key realization, and probably the most fundamental part of this particular lecture. In real world situations, not all incorrect or correct matches hold equal value, which is why we actually have those various classification metrics. It's not enough to understand that you got a particular count of correct versus a particular count of incorrect. It's various ratios that we need to take into account. So, in the real world a single metric won't tell the complete story.

So to understand all of this we're gonna bring back those four metrics we mentioned at the beginning, and see how they're actually calculated. And we could actually organize our predicted values compared to the real values, and what's known as a confusion matrix. And then in the next lecture we're gonna have a whole discussion of the confusion matrix. But for right now, let's look at accuracy. Accuracy in classification problems is the number of correct predictions made by the model divided by the total number of predictions.

So again, what we do, is we count up how many times the model was correct and then we divide it by the total number of predictions the model made. For example, if the test dataset was 100 messages and our model correctly predicted 80 of those messages from the test data set, then we would have 80 out of a total of 100 predictions that were done correctly. And that means we were 0.8 or 80% accurate. Accuracy is really useful when target classes are well balanced.

So, in that example, it would mean that we have roughly the same amount of spam messages in our test data as we have ham, or legitimate messages. Now, here's a problem. Accuracy is not a good choice with unbalanced classes. Let's imagine a situation where we had 99 legitimate ham messages and one spam text message. If our model was a simple line of code that's always predicted ham, that means we would get 99% accuracy, even though it was incorrect on every single instance of spam.

We got unlucky because we had an unbalanced class situation in our test data set. So in this situation, we're gonna wanna understand recall and precision, that's going to give us a better understanding of how it performs on the other smaller class. So let's quickly go over some formal definitions of precision, recall and F1 score. F1 score is simply a combination of precision and recall. We're gonna go over their formal definitions, and I think usually they make a lot more sense when you see them in combination of the confusion matrix.

So keep in mind, in the next lecture you'll see a confusion matrix which should clear up precision, recall, and F1 score with further detail. Let's start with recall. Recall is the ability of a machine learning model to find all the relevant cases within a data set. And the actual mathematical formula for definition of recall is going to be the number of true positives, divided by the number of true positives plus the number of false negatives. And when we see this in combination of the confusion matrix they'll make a lot more sense.

So keep in mind, in the next lecture we're actually gonna see the formulas and how they're calculated. So don't worry too much about these formal definitions. Precision is the ability of a classification model to identify only the relevant data points. Precision is defined as a number of true positives divided by the number of true positives plus the number of false positives. And in the next lecture we'll talk more about true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives.

So keep in mind, often you have a trade off between recall and precision. While recall expresses the ability to find all relevant instances in a data set, precision expresses the proportion of the data points our model says was relevant, that actually were relevant. And in cases where we want to find an optimal blend of precision and recall, we can combine the two metrics using what is called the F1 score. The F1 score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall taking both metrics into account in the following equation.

We say F1 score is equal to two times, and in the numerator we have precision times recall, and then in the denominator we have precision plus recall. And this is known as taking a harmonic mean instead of just a normal mean. And the reason we use the harmonic mean instead of a simple average is because it punishes extreme values. Let's imagine we had a classifier with a precision of 1.0 but a horrible recall of 0.0. If we were to perform the normal mean we would end up getting an F1 score of 0.5.

So, that's just a simple average. If we take the harmonic mean, we can be a little smarter about it and really punish this model for having a horrible recall, because we're gonna say precision times recall, then that's gonna say one time zero is zero. So it means the F1 score automatically becomes very small, or zero, if one of these values, either precision or recall, happens to be very poor. So again, it's a better display of the optimal blend than just a simple average, because it's punishing extreme values.

So precision recall, as I keep mentioning, typically make more sense in the context of a confusion matrix. So in this next lecture coming up we're gonna explore this confusion matrix. And again, talk again in more detail about the different classification metrics we'll be using throughout this section of the course. We'll see you at the next lecture.

42. Text Feature Extraction Overview English
=============================================

(soft meditative music) -: Welcome back, everyone, to this lecture on feature extraction from text. This is part one where we're going to be going over the basic theory of feature extraction. Most classic machine learning algorithms can't actually accept or take in raw text. Instead, we need to perform some sort of feature extraction from the raw text in order to pass numerical features to the machine learning algorithm. For example, we could count the occurrence of each word to map text to an actual number.

Let's discuss count vectorization along with term frequency and inverse document frequency. So count vectorization looks like this. Let's imagine we had a list of messages. So here we have three messages. And this looks really similar to the data set we previously had. So right now everything is in raw text or a string in Python. What we can do is using Scikit-learn, we can perform count vectorization. And notice this syntax here looks really similar to the same way we created machine learning models of Scikit-learn.

We say from SK-learn, dot, and then some family of feature extraction models, specifically from text. We import this count vectorizer. And what this is going to do is it's going to count the occurrences of all the unique words. So after you've created your count vectorizer, you're going to vectorize those messages. And basically what happens is it treats each individual unique word as a feature. And then it's going to count the occurrence of each unique feature or word throughout every single document.

And each document is essentially just each text message. So we can think of the term document as just another word for every document text. So if we take a look here, notice that the word call didn't show up in the first message or the third message. It only showed up in the middle message. Or the word dogs only shows up in the last message. Now, some words do show up more than once, like the word go and the word to. But overall, we should see a lot of zeros, because most messages are not gonna contain all the words.

So you can imagine for a very large set of documents, otherwise known as a corpus, we're gonna have what's known as a very sparse matrix, a matrix of a lot of zeros. This sort of matrix is known as the document term matrix, or DTM. Again, we're just counting the number of times each unique word throughout the entire vocabulary of all the documents shows up in each particular document. Now, an alternative to count vectorization is something called TF-IDF vectorizer, or term frequency inverse document frequency vectorizer.

It's also going to create a document term matrix from our messages. However, instead of filling in the document term matrix, or DTM, with token counts, it calculates term frequency inverse document frequency value for each word. So let's talk about what that actually means. What does TF-IDF mean? Well, let's first look at that first term, TF, or term frequency. We can think of term frequency as a function of the term and the particular document. And all it is, it's the raw count of a term in a document.

Basically just answering the question the number of times that that particular term occurs in Document D. So for example, if we take a look at the term frequency for the word dogs, the term frequency for the first two messages would be zero. And then the term frequency for the word dogs in the last message would be one. Again, the term frequency is just the raw count of a term in a document, something that we just saw. However, term frequency alone isn't enough for a thorough feature analysis of the text.

Let's imagine very common terms, like stop words such as a or the. Because the terms such as the is so common, term frequency will tend to incorrectly emphasize documents which happen to use words like the more frequently, without giving enough weight to the more meaningful terms, like unique words such as red and dogs. An inverse document frequency factor is incorporated, which diminishes the weight of terms that occur very frequently in the document set and increases the weight of the terms that occur rarely.

It is the logarithmically scaled inverse fraction of the documents that contain the word obtained by dividing the total number of documents by the number of documents containing the term, and then taking the logarithmic of that quotient. So we know that TF-IDF is then the term frequency times one over the document frequency, otherwise known as the inverse document frequency. So here we can see the formula for term frequency inverse document frequency, as well as the full equation for inverse document frequency.

Now, fortunately for us, Scikit-learn can calculate all these terms for us through the use of its API. Keep in mind, you should notice how similar the syntax is to our previous use of machine learning models and Scikit-learn. Here we can see that from sklearn.feature_extraction.text, we're able to import the TF-IDF vectorizer. And again, we simply call TF-IDF vectorizer, and in this case we're going to call fit transform on the actual raw text messages, which is going to perform term frequency inverse document frequency vectorization on the actual text messages.

So all this is doing is not only is it taking into account the term frequency, how many times the terms show up in a single document, but as well as the inverse document frequency. How often do these terms show up across all the documents? That way very common words aren't weighed as much as the unique words. TF-IDF allows us to understand the context of words across an entire corpus of documents instead of just its relative importance in a single document.

Coming up next, we're going to explore how to perform these operations with Python and Scikit-learn. We'll see it next lecture.

It can perform both the vectorization and the classification. So instead of having to do this sort of fit transform and count vectorization on your test data in order to call predict what we can do is actually combine everything we just saw into one single pipeline step. So here I'm going to pass in a list of tuples and each tuple, what it's going to have is going to have a string name that you decide on what to call this step in the pipeline. So I'm going to say TFIDF is my first step of the pipeline and then you actually call what you want to occur. So I can pass in my vectorizer object there as the first item in my tuple. Then I can pass in another tuple pair. So I can say I actually perform the classification using a linear svc.

Notice my use of open and close parenthesis here and there we can actually create our pipeline. So this pipeline object behaves just like a normal classifier of scikit-learn or a normal machine learning model. However, what's convenient about this pipeline object is that it actually can perform all these steps for you in a single call. Then that means you can directly provide the original X training data and have it be both vectorized and run the classifier on it in a single step.

So this is what you see often used, especially in natural language processing, where you can have a really long pipeline of things like text feature extraction, removing stop words, tokenization, lemmatization, et cetera. So the pipeline is a really convenient way to do this. Right now we have a very kind of short pipeline. ===================================================

Instructor: Welcome back, everyone. Let's now discuss semantics and word vectors. But first, in order to actually use spaCy's embedded word vectors, we must download the larger spaCy English models. Full details about the models and as well as how to install them can be found at the link right here, spacy.io/usage/models. Recall that when we first installed spaCy not only did we have to do an environmental install either using conda install or PIP install, but we also at the command line had to install the full English language models.

Now, if you went ahead and downloaded the default model that would be the small model and that actually doesn't contain the word vectors because the word vectors themselves is actually a large enough piece of data that spaCy has medium size and larger size models for that. So in order to download either the medium or large spaCy English models at your command line you need to type out Python, space, dash M space spaCy, space, download, space, and then en underscore core underscore web underscore and then either MD for the medium English model, or LG for the large spaCy English model.

And the difference between medium and large for the context of these lectures doesn't really make a huge difference. So you can choose either one. I went ahead and download the large English model. Just keep in mind these are really large files so they may take a while to download. So run either of these two lines at your command line in order to install the medium or large spaCy English models. And again, you can check out the link at usage slash models for full details on this installation process.

So now that you have the larger models that contain the actual word vectors, let's discuss how word vectors are created. word2vec is a two layer neural net that processes text. Its input is a text corpus, and its output is a set of vectors, which are essentially just feature vectors for words in that corpus. The purpose and usefulness of word2vec is to group the vectors of similar words together in this vector space. And that is it's able to detect similarities mathematically.

And we'll discuss that in further detail in just a little bit. But first, try to understand that word2vec is really just creating vectors that are distributed numerical representations of word features, features such as the context of individual words. And what's really interesting, it's able to do all of this without any sort of human intervention. It's just learning naturally from a very large corpus of text the context of different words and which words are similar to other words.

So theoretically given enough data, usage and context. So we're dealing for really large corpus, for example, all of Wikipedia, once you feed that into the word2vec algorithm, word2vec can make highly accurate guesses about a words meaning based on past appearances and the context surrounding that particular word. So those guesses can then be used to establish a words association with other words. So after you actually train a word2vec model the vectors themselves will carry information and it's gonna be able to understand associations such as man is to boy, as woman is to girl.

So it will understand that there's some sort of relationship of age there that man and woman tend to be the older words you use versus boy and girl tend to be the younger words you use. Now, word2vec trains words against other words that neighbor them in the input corpus. And it can actually do this one of two ways either using context to predict a target word. And this is known as a continuous bag of words approach or C-B-O-W or cbow. Or the other method that's also common is using a word to predict a target context, which is called skip gram.

So here we see a diagram of the two possible approaches and basically they're kind of inverses of each other. For the CBOW approach, you have several input words and then your projection is essentially trying to predict what is the highest probability word to show up given the context of those surrounding words. Now, the skip gram method takes a little longer to train and to develop because it's essentially doing the opposite given an input of a single word using the auto encoder neural network projection try to output the weighted probabilities of the other words that are gonna show up around the context of this input word.

So again, you have kind of two inverse methods here either given the context words and then predicting the output word or inputting the word and then trying to predict the output context words. So at the end of all this, recall that each word is now going to be represented by a vector. And in spaCy, each of these vectors has 300 dimensions while you're actually training the autoencoder, if you were to implement word2vec yourself, granted it takes a very long time on a very large corpus.

So it's unusual for people to actually not use some sort of built in embedded word vectors. But if you did want to actually train your own auto encoder for word2vec, then you could actually theoretically choose either less or more dimensions. Typically, the ranges are between 100 to 1000 dimensions. The higher number of dimensions the longer the training time. But you should have then more context around each of these words since you have more dimensions to hold more information.

Now what this means is since we have each word mapped to a vector in this 300 dimensional space we can use cosine similarity to measure how similar word vectors are to each other. So cosine similarity is essentially just checking the distance between two vectors. And here we see a simple diagram in a two dimensional space, but this expands out to n dimensions. And in our case, we'll be taking, several 300 dimensional vectors and then calculating the cosine similarity between them to see what vectors are most similar to each other.

And in this case, the actual vectors represent words. Now what's really interesting is this means we can now perform vector arithmetic with the new word vectors. So we can calculate a brand new vector by performing arithmetic that is adding or subtracting different vectors. So I can take the vector, that 300 dimensional vector for king, and then subtract the vectors for man and then add the vector for a woman. So that creates a brand new vector that's not actually directly associated with a single word.

Instead, we performed an arithmetic between the vectors of several words and then what I can attempt to do is find the most similar existing vectors to this new vector. So hopefully after you do something like king minus man plus woman, that new vector it's closest existing word vector could be something like queen. Essentially understanding the context of royalty along one dimension and then moving along another dimension for gender. So this is able to actually establish really interesting relationships between the word vectors including relationships between male versus female like we just explained earlier or even dimensions for verb tense.

So we can understand that walking is to walk as swimming is to swim. So let's begin to explore spaCy word vectors with python. I'll see you at the next lecture.

51. Sentiment Analysis Overview English
========================================

(bright music) -: Welcome back, everyone. In this lecture, we're going to discuss the general idea behind VADER sentiment analysis with Python and NLTK. We've already explored text classification and using it to predict sentiment labels on pre-labeled movie reviews. That means someone has already read the review for us, and manually labeled it either positive or negative. But what happens if we don't already have those labels? Are there pre-constructed methods of attempting to discern sentiment, sentiment being whether you feel positive about the text or negative about the text or neutral, on raw unlabeled text?

Is there a way we can take that raw text data and pass it through some sort of pre-built algorithm that will directly declare the sentiment for us without even needing any sort of pre-labeled training mechanisms? Well, luckily, one of the solutions is called VADER, which stands for Valence Aware Dictionary for sEntiment Reasoning. So the sentiment, the E is capitalized there 'cause that's where they get the E for VADER. And VADER is a model used for text sentiment analysis that is sensitive to both polarity, that is whether something is positive or negative, and intensity, that is the strength of the emotion.

And we'll talk about strength in a little bit. Now, luckily for us, it is available in the NLTK package and can be applied directly to unlabeled text data. You do have to download it but it's a simple download call from within NLTK, so keep that in mind, if you have a firewall, you may need to disable it in order to download. And we cover the download process in the very next lecture. Now, primarily, VADER sentiment analysis relies on a dictionary which maps lexical features to emotion intensities, called sentiment scores.

The sentiment score of a text can then be attained by summing up the intensity of each word in the text. So essentially, every single word has some sort of positive or negative value attached to it. And what we do is we just take all the words inside of a text document, and then convert them into positive or negative values, and then we sum that all up and that will be our document sentiment score. So for example, you can think of words like love, like, enjoy, and happy, those all convey a positive sentiment.

And remember, we also talked about the strength of the emotion, and VADER is actually intelligent enough to understand basic context of these words, such as "did not love" as a negative sentiment. So don't be too concerned about things like having the word "love" be completely positive because VADER will also take into account the context of the words, because if there's a negative word, such as "not", preceding a positive word, then you can understand that that whole sentiment is probably negative.

So not love or did not like, did not enjoy, VADER will understand that. And it's also going to understand things like capitalization and punctuation. So if you write just a lowercase word "love", that doesn't convey a positive emotion as strongly as "love" in all caps with a bunch of exclamation marks. So VADER's also smart enough to take that into account. Now, sentiment on raw text is always going to be challenging because there's a variety of possible factors, things like positive and negative sentiment in the same text data.

So for example, if you're reading a movie review, often, movie reviews will praise actors' capabilities, however, maybe they judge the overall screenplay or the script of the movie itself as very poor. And as we know, if you've seen a movie with great actors in it that isn't a good movie, often has to do with the screenplay or the poor script or just the poor storyline. So a movie review could have positive things about the actors, but then negative things to say about the overall movie itself.

And if there's more positive things about the actors, then VADER's going to say that the overall movie review could be positive, when in fact the true analysis of the movie review when written by human would be that the overall movie was negative but the actors were positive in it. Now another thing that's very difficult to detect is sarcasm. This is pretty much impossible for VADER to detect because you end up using positive words in a negative way sarcastically.

So keep these two ideas in mind. This sentiment analysis from VADER is definitely not gonna be foolproof. So what we're gonna do now is explore using VADER sentiment analysis, NLTK, and Python. The key thing to keep in mind here is that we're going to be able to apply VADER onto any raw text data that we want. Okay, let's get started. I'll see you in the next lecture.

57. Overview of Topic Modeling English
=======================================

(gentle chiming music) -: Welcome back. Let's quickly discuss an overview of what topic modeling is before we actually dive into the methods of topic modeling. So we're gonna learn about Latent Dirichlet allocation and non-negative matrix factorization. But first, let's understand a general topic of topic modeling. Topic modeling allows us to efficiently analyze large volumes of texts by clustering documents together into topics. A large amount of text data is actually unlabeled, meaning we won't be able to apply our previous supervised learning approaches because those machine learning models would actually depend on historical label data.

And in the real world, specifically for text data, you're not gonna have a convenient label attached to a text data set, such as positive or negative, or spam versus ham. Instead, you may have a variety of labels such as the different categories that a newspaper article could be in and you may just have the text itself unlabeled. So it's up to us to try to discover those labels through topic modeling. So again, if we have unlabeled data then we can attempt to discover these labels.

And in the case of text data this means really attempting to discover clusters of similar documents grouped together hopefully by some sort of topic. A very important idea to keep in mind here is that it's actually very difficult to evaluate an unsupervised learning model's effectiveness because we didn't actually know the correct topic or the right answer to begin with. All we know is that the documents clustered together share some sort of similar topic ideas.

It's up to the user to identify what these topics actually represent. So again, these unsupervised learning algorithms, there's not really a good way to evaluate how well they did because we never really had a right answer to begin with. We're gonna begin by examining how Latent Dirichlet allocation works and how it can attempt to discover topics for a corpus of documents. Let's get started. I'll see you at the next lecture.

58. Latent Dirichlet Allocation Overview English
=================================================

(intro music) -: Welcome back everyone. In this lecture, we're going to give an explanatory overview of how LDA or Latent Dirichlet allocation for topic modeling works. Johann Dirichlet was a German mathematician in the 1800s who contributed widely to the field of modern mathematics. And there's a probability distribution named after him called the Dirichlet Distribution. And this is the distribution that is used later on in LDA. So Latent Dirichlet allocation is based off this probability distribution.

And in 2003, LDA was actually first published as a graphical model for topic discovery in the journal of Machine Learning Research. So keep in mind, even though Dirichlet's name is attached to this particular method for a topic modeling, it really just stems from the fact that it uses the Dirichlet probability distribution, not that Dirichlet himself actually invented LDA for topic modeling. That actual method is relatively new, from 2003. So we're gonna get a high level overview of how LDA works for topic modeling but I would really encourage you to also take a look at the original publication paper.

Now there are two main assumptions we're going to make in order to actually apply LDA for topic modeling. The first one is that documents with similar topics use similar groups of words. And that's a pretty reasonable assumption because that basically is saying that if you have various documents covering a similar topic, like, a bunch of documents covering the topic of business or economy, that they should end up using similar words like money, price, market, stocks, et cetera.

The other assumption we're going to make is that latent topics can then be found by searching for groups of words that frequently occur together in documents across the corpus. And that's gonna be the assumption that we're really gonna dive into the details later on. So again, these are the two assumptions and they're both actually quite reasonable for the way humans write documents. And we can actually think of these two assumptions mathematically. The way we can model these assumptions is the following.

We can say that documents are probability distributions over some underlying latent topics. And then topics themselves are probability distributions over words. So let's see how each of those actually plays out. We can imagine that any particular document is going to have a probability distribution over a given amount of latent topics. So let's say, we decide that there are five latent topics across various documents. Then any particular document is going to have a probability of belonging to each topic.

So here we can see document one has the highest probability of belonging to topic number two. So we have this discrete probability distribution across the topics for each document. Then we could look at another document such as document number two and in this case, it does have probabilities of belonging to other topics but we're gonna say that it has the highest probability of belonging to topic four. Notice here, we're not saying definitively that document one belongs to any particular topic or document two belongs to any particular topic.

Instead, we're modeling them as having a probability distribution over a variety of latent topics. And then if we look at the topics themselves, those are simply going to be modeled as probability distributions over words. So for example, we can define topic one as different probabilities belonging to each of these words as belonging to that topic. So, we can see here that it has a low probability of the word He belonging at topic one, low probability of Food belonging at topic one, et cetera.

And then we can see that words such as Cat and Dog have a higher probability of belonging to topic one. And here is where we're actually going to begin as a user trying to understand what this topic is actually representative of. So, if we were to get this sort of probability distribution across all the vocabulary of all the words in the corpus, what we would end up doing is asking for maybe the top 10 highest probability words for topic one. And then we would try to realize what the actual underlying topic was.

So in this case, we could make an educated guess that topic one happens to do with pets and we would say that topic one has to do with pets. Again, the LDA or unsupervised learning technique is not going to be able to tell you that directly. It's up to the user to interpret these probability distributions as topics. And we'll actually get hands on practice with that when we perform LDA ourselves with Python. So LDA represents documents as mixtures of topics that spit out words with certain probabilities.

And it's going to assume that documents are produced in the following fashion. It's first going to decide on the number of words, N, that the document will have. Then, we choose a topic mixture for the document. According to a Dirichlet distribution over a fixed set of K topics. So, that's where the Dirichlet distribution comes to effect. So for example, we start off and say this document is 60% business, 20% politics and 10% foods. So, that's our actual distribution.

And then what we're going to do is we're gonna generate each word in the document by first picking a topic according to the multinomial distribution that we sampled previously. So we picked words, 60% of them from the business topic, 20% of them from politics and then 10% from the food topic. And then using the topic to generate the word itself. So again, this is according to the topic's own multinomial distribution across the words. So for example, if we selected the food topic, we might generate the word Apple with 60% probability and another word Home with less probability, like 30% probability and so on.

Assuming this sort of generative model for a collection of documents, LDA is actually going to then try to backtrack from the documents to find the set of topics that are likely to have generated the collection. So again, this process here that we just went over, LDA is assuming that that's how you built the documents. Now, obviously in the real world, you're not actually building documents with this sort of frame of mind, but it's a very useful construct of the way topics can be mixed throughout various documents and the way words can be mixed throughout various topics.

So, what we're gonna do is attempt to backtrack that sort of process. So, let's actually show you what LDA is going to do since it's assuming that that's how you built the documents. So we're gonna imagine we have a set of documents and the first step we have to do is actually choose some fixed number of K topics to discover. And you should note that very carefully that this is actually really hard. In order for LDA to work, you as a user need to decide how many topics are going to be discovered.

So even before you start LDA, you need to have some sort of intuition over how many topics. So, we choose some fixed number K of topics to discover and then we're going to want to use LDA to learn the topic representation of each document and the words associated to each topic. Then we're going to go through each document and we're going to randomly assign each word in the document to one of the K topics. So keep in mind, the very first pass, this random assignment actually already gives you both topic representations of all the documents and word distributions of all the topics.

And we've assigned everything randomly at the very first pass. So, we're technically not done yet because these initial random topics won't really make sense. They're gonna be really poor representations of topics since you essentially just assigned every word a random topic. So, now it's time to iterate over this and see how we can figure out how to fix these sort of assignments. So we're gonna iterate over every word in every document to improve these topics.

And what we're going to do is for every word in every document, and for each topic, T, we're going to calculate the following. We're gonna calculate the proportion of words in document D that are currently assigned to topic T. Then we're also going to calculate the proportion of assignments to topic T over all documents that come from this particular word W. And then we're going to reassign W a new topic where we choose topic T with probability of topic T given document D times probability of word W given topic T.

So, this is essentially the probability that topic T generated the word W. And after repeating that previous step a large number of times, we eventually reach a roughly steady state where the assignments for the are acceptable. These words and topics don't start changing that often; They become pretty steady. So at the end, what we have is each document being assigned to a topic. And then what we can do is we can then search for the words that have the highest probability of being assigned a topic.

So we end up with an output such as this. We'll say, after running through all the documents and performing LDA, you pass in one particular document and it'll report back, LDA will report back, I think this document is assigned to topic number four. And we actually don't know yet, what topic number four represents but what we can ask LDA is what are the most common words in topic four or what words have the highest probability of showing up in topic four?

And then you may get results like Cat Vet, Birds, Dog, Food, Home, et cetera. And then, given this list of high probability words for this particular topic, it's up to the user to interpret what topic this is. And for this, I think a reasonable interpretation would be that these particular documents assigned to topic four probably have to do something with pets. And we would say, okay, I think topic four is pets. Now, is that the right answer? There's really no way of knowing because we didn't have the right answer to begin with.

But, it's a reasonable assumption to make given these high probability of words showing up for topic four. So again, two important notes here before we continue and actually apply this with Python. The first important note is that the user themselves must decide on the amount of topics present in the document before even beginning this process. And the second one is that the user themselves must interpret what the topics are and what they're actually representing.

Okay, in the next lecture, we're gonna explore how to actually implement LDA with Python and scikit-learn. I'll see you there.

61. Nonnegative Matrix Factorization Overview English
======================================================

(calming music) -: Welcome back everyone. In this lecture we're going to discuss non-negative matrix factorization and how we can apply it to topic modeling. Non-negative matrix factorization is an unsupervised algorithm that simultaneously performs dimensionality reduction and clustering. We can use it in conjunction with TF-IDF to model topics across various documents. So let's discuss the general mathematics behind non-negative matrix factorization or NMF and then we'll show you how you can apply it with a document term matrix.

So first, you're given a non-negative matrix A which is what we're going to be able to create using TF-IDF. And then we want to find a K dimension approximation in terms of two other non-negative factors, W and H. So you're gonna have some matrix A, which is N by M, some rows features and columns or objects. And we wanna perform factorization to essentially approximate A with the matrix multiplication of W and H, where we have N by K for W, and K by M for H.

And again, we are going to approximate each object. That is the columns of A by a linear combination of K reduced dimensions or basis vectors in W. Each basis vector can then be interpreted as a cluster. And the memberships of objects in these clusters are encoded by H. So the way this is going to work again, is we have an input that we're providing, a non-negative data matrix which is A. And in this case, that's going to be your TF-IDF. So your Term Frequency- Inverse Document Frequency for the documents versus the actual words in those, across all the documents.

Then, we're going to have a number of basis vectors or K, In our case for topic modeling, the K is going to be the number of topics we choose. So just like before with LDA, we actually have to decide on how many topics we want to try to discover. So we get to choose the value of K. And then, we're going to initialize some values for the factors W and H. And these start off as random matrices. Then, it's our job to essentially, first, get some sort of measurement of the reconstruction error between A and the approximation WH.

And then what we're going to do is optimize. So we have an expectation maximization optimization to essentially refine W and H in order to minimize that objective function. Essentially, keep updating those coefficients until the approximation of WH is actually going to make sense for A. So a common approach is to essentially iterate between two multiplicative update rules until convergence. Updating H, then updating W and then repeating that process until it converges.

So the way this works for our particular use case is we first construct a vector space model documents after you do things like filter out stop words. And that results in a term document matrix for A, or a document term matrix DTM. Then, we apply TF-IDF term weight normalization to A and then normalize TF-IDF vectors to unit length. Keep in mind, those first three steps, even though it sounds like a lot, scikit-learn is essentially doing all of that for us when we call TF-IDF vectorizer.

So we don't have to do things like remove the stop words ourselves, and call count vectorizer, and then call the term frequency, and then figure out inverse document frequency. Recall that all of that from text feature extraction is essentially done under the hood for us by calling the TF-IDF vectorizer with scikit-learn. So once we've done that, what we're going to do is we're going to initialize the factors using NNDSVD or non-negative double singular value decomposition on the A matrix.

And then we're going to apply a projected gradient non-negative matrix factorization to A. So then what we end up discovering are the basis vectors. Those are the topics or clusters in the data. And then we have a coefficient matrix which is the membership weights for documents relative to each topic. In this case, each cluster. So again, all we're doing is we're going to create a document term matrix with TF-IDF vectorization. So we've done that before.

It looks something like this here. Instead of numerical values, we're just representing the various values by how dark a blue there is. And then we're gonna have a resulting W and H. And so the basis vectors, those columns in W, those are your topics. And we can see how words relate to particular topics. So it looks like topic one has to do with TV shows and actors. Topic two has to do with sports clubs, footballs. And then topic three tends to have to do with business: banks, moneys and finance.

And then H is going to have coefficients, and that's going to be the memberships for each document. So we have coefficient values for each document and how they relate to each topic. So the results are pretty similar as far as interpretation to what we saw before. We get some sort of relationship between the documents and the topics, and the words and the topics. Now keep in mind, just like LDA, we're still going to need to select the number of expected topics beforehand.

So we need to actually again choose that value of K and say something like "I believe there are 12 topics across all these articles." Or "I believe there are seven topics." So you have to initialize with some sort of value of K. And also just like with LDA, we will have to interpret these topics based off the coefficient values of the words per topic. Keep in mind, these matrix coefficient values are not probabilities that can be interpreted like we did with LDA.

We're still going to be able to interpret a relationship between a word to a topic with the coefficient, and between a document to a topic with the coefficient, but it's not like a probability value like LDA gave us. It's just a coefficient value that the nonnegative matrix factorization solved for. Now, luckily due to scikit-learn uniform syntax, switching out LDA for NMF is actually very simple. In fact, we're gonna be able to kind of run through the last notebook and very easily adapt it for NMF.

So we're going to repeat our analysis of the NPR article data set and show you how you can do topic modeling and discover topics NMF. And we're gonna be able to go through it a lot faster because we actually just have to swap out a couple of things in order to run NMF. So we're gonna use the same notebook as we did before and then just show you what you need to swap to perform non-negative matrix factorization. Okay, we'll see you at the next lecture.

And it can be multiple inputs. So it can aggregate them. And then once it aggregates those inputs, it passes it through some sort of activation function. Here we're just using the symbol for a rectified linear unit, but it can be things like a sigmoid function, or a tanh function, et cetera.

And then from that we have an output. So that's how a normal neuron works. A recurrent neuron is a little different. What it's going to do is send the output back to itself. So the output goes back into input of the same neuron. So we can actually unroll this throughout time. So if we unroll this throughout time, it ends up looking something like this. So we can see time here kind of represented on the X-axis. And we have this particular recurrent neuron where it's input at T minus one gives an output at T minus one.

And then that gets passed into the neuron. And it's an input at time T. And then that has an output at time T. And then we can take that output and pass it in as input for the same neuron at time T plus one. And then so on and so on. So this is called unrolling that recurrent neuron. Something that's important to note here is that the neuron is actually receiving both inputs from a previous time step as well as inputs from the current time step. So you can see each of these neurons has two sets of inputs there.

These cells that are a function of input from previous time steps are also known as memory cells. And recurrent neural networks are also flexible in their inputs and outputs for both sequences and single vector values. So we'll show a couple of examples of that in just a little bit, but I want you to know it's also very easy to create a layer of recurrent neurons. Previously, we just saw one recurrent neuron. Then we unrolled it through time, but we can do the same thing for an entire layer.

So if we want an entire layer of recurrent neurons, then it would look something like this where we have input of X and then it goes through those recurrent neurons. So here we have three of them. Then we see the output at Y. And then we take that output and then just pass it back in to all the neurons in that layer. And we could do the same idea and unroll this entire layer throughout time so that we get an input at T equals zero, get an output at T equals zero, and then pass that along with the output and input at time plus one.

And same idea, except now, we're doing it with an entire layer. Now, since the output of these recurrent neurons at a time step T is technically a function of all the inputs from previous time steps, you could then begin to think that it has some form of memory because we're technically passing in historical information into that recurrent neuron or into that layer of recurrent neurons. So part of this neural network that preserves some sort of state across these time steps is called a memory cell.

And later on, we're gonna see much more complex examples of memory cells, but for now, we have this basic recurrent neuron that just sends its output back into its input. Recurrent neural networks are also very flexible in their inputs and outputs. So let's walk through a few examples of different combinations of inputs and outputs we can use for recurrent neural networks. So we can actually perform a sequence input to a sequence output. And an example of that would be passing in a set of time series information, such as a year's worth of daily sales data.

And then wanting back a sequence of that same sales data shifted over a certain time period into the future. And that's basically that initial example we first thought of. That one, two, three, four, five. And then you ask back for two, three, four, five, six. So a sequence, however, shifted over one step into the future. Another set of examples, inputs and outputs, would be to pass in a sequence input and request back a vector output. So that's usually indicated by one versus they really dislike the movie, which is indicated by usually negative one or zero. So that would be an example of a sequence input. So a single input at just a first time step and basically pass in zeros for the rest of your time steps. And then let the output be a sequence. So that's a vector to sequence network. So an example of vector to sequence could be just providing a single seed word and then getting out an entire sequence of high-probability sequence phrases that would actually come out.
