-: Welcome back everyone to this lecture on LSTM and GRU. So a common issue that recurrent neural networks face is that after a while, especially if you're training the network on a really large sequence, then the recurrent neural network is going to begin to forget those very first inputs. The later and later training batches of later inputs, things that come towards the end of the text document, that's gonna start kind of overriding the weights of the very beginning inputs of the beginning of the text.

So what we wanna make sure is that we're not beginning to forget those first inputs as information is lost at each step going through the recurrent neural network. So what we really need is we need some sort of long term memory for our networks. We need to balance both the short term memory of the networks, the data that it was recently trained on, versus the long term memory of the networks, all the data and the very first data that was being trained on.

So the LSTM, or long short-term memory cell, was created to help address these recurrent neural network issues. And while long short-term memory cells aren't exactly state of the art, they're still quite new, considering that they've really only been around in Keras for just the past few years. So what we're gonna do is go through how an LSTM cell works. Keep in mind there's actually a lot of mathematics here. This is really just a mathematical theory lecture.

When we're actually dealing with Keras, it's gonna be a simple import call. So check out the resource link for a full really great breakdown. In fact, it's a super common link when discussing LSTMs. And then we'll also discuss the related GRU cell. But for our use case, I really want you to focus on LSTM because that's what's most commonly used for text generation. So let's get started learning how this LSTM cell actually works. But quickly recall that a typical recurrent neuron basically has this sort of structure, where we have some input at T minus one, then we have the output at T minus one, and that output is also fed back in along with input at T.

So, as a quick note, these outputs are often called hidden. So we can have, instead of just saying output at T minus one, we can say H of T minus one. And then that also gets fed in with the input at T. That gets passed through some sort of activation function, like the hyperbolic tangent. Then that gives the output H of T, et cetera. So when you see H of T, just kind of think of that as a typical output of a recurrent neuron cell. All right, so here we can see the entire model of a long short-term memory cell.

And I know it can look really complicated if you see it in this format, but it's actually not so bad when you break it down in parts. So let's go ahead and first take a look at what's being inputted and what's being outputted. Here, we still have those original inputs from a normal recurrent neuron, those H of T minus one and X of T. But note here we have this third input, and we'll call that the cell state. So right now we're receiving the cell state at T minus one, and then with the outputs, we again output H of T.

And then we also have the cell state. So that's the current cell state, C of T. So what we're gonna end up doing is we're gonna take in H of T minus one and X of T, as well as the previous cell state, C of T minus one, and we'll output H of T as well as the current cell state, C of T. So we're gonna do this step by step. So the very first step is called the forget gates layer. And this is gonna be the first step where we decide what information are we going to forget or throw away from the cell state.

So what we end up doing here is we pass in H of T minus one and X of T, and we pass that in after performing a linear transformation with some weights and bias terms into a sigmoid function. And remember, because this is essentially a sigmoid layer, it's always going to output a number between zero and one. And a one is going to represent to keep it, and a zero is going to represent to forget about it, or get rid of it. So if we think back to maybe a language model where we're trying to predict the very next word based on previous ones, a cell state might include the gender of the present subject, so that you end up picking the correct pronoun.

So when you end up seeing a new subject, you wanna forget about the gender of the old subject. So that may be a use for a forget gate layer when working with a natural language as a sequence. Now the very next step is to decide what new information are you going to store in the cell state. So we already decided what we're going to forget. Now we need to decide what are we actually going to store in the cell state. Remember that's C of T. So the first part is a sigmoid layer, and the second part is a hyperbolic tangent layer.

So let's go ahead and tackle that first part, that sigmoid layer. So that sigmoid layer is called the input gate layer. So we'll say that's equal to I of T for input gate layer. And again, we take in H of T minus one and X of T, do a linear transformation on it with W of I plus B of I. We pass that into a sigmoid function. And again, now we have a bunch of values between zero and ones. Then the second part of this is the hyperbolic tangent layer. And that's again, going to take H of T minus one and X of T, do that linear transformation, and then pass it through a hyperbolic tangent.

So that ends up creating a vector of what we call new candidate values. So that's that C of T with a little tilde above it. So these are candidate values that could be added to the state. And in the next step, we're going to combine these two to create an update to the cell state. So if we think back to an example of a language model, we essentially want to add the gender of the new subject to the cell state and replace the old one that we've already decided we're forgetting.

So now it's time to update the old cell state. So remember, the old cell state is C of T minus one, and we eventually want to update that to the new cell state, C of T, so we can pass that on to the T plus one state of the cell. So in the previous steps, we already decided what we're gonna forget, and we also already decided what we're going to store. So now what we need to do is just perform or execute these actions. So what we end up doing is we multiply the old state, C of T minus one by F of T.

So we end up forgetting the things that we essentially decided we're gonna forget, based off that first sigmoid layer. Then we're going to add I of T, that input gate layer, times those candidate values, C of T with the tilde on top. So these are the new candidate values, and now they're being scaled by how much we decided to update each state value. So if we think back to a case of a language model, this is where we would actually drop the information about that old subject's gender and add in new information, based off what we decided in the previous steps.

Now our final decision is going to be what do we output for H of T? So this output is gonna be based off your cell state, but it's just a filtered version. So it's actually pretty straightforward now. We're just using H of T minus one and X of T, we pass that after a linear transformation into the sigmoid layer, and that's going to decide what parts of the cell state we're going to be outputting. Then, we put the cell state through a hyperbolic tangent.

So that's gonna push the values to be between negative one and one, and we're going to then multiply it by the output of that sigmoid gate so that we only output the parts we decided to. So that's what's occurring here in this red line. Again, we're taking H of T minus one and X of T, doing a linear transformation on it, passing it through the sigmoid, and then once we have that output, so we're gonna say that's O of T, output of T, we're then going to multiply it by the hyperbolic tangent of the C of T, or that current cell state.

And that gives us H of T. So that's how an LSTM cell works. Now there's different variants on an LSTM cell. So there's a variance called the peephole variant. So this is actually quite popular, and the reason for that is because it adds peepholes to all the gates. So basically it allows these F of T, I of T, and O of T, to be able to see the previous cell state, or C of T minus one. So you can see here, now instead of being a function of just H of T minus one and X of T, we're also passing in C of T minus one.

And then for the output, we're also passing in a C of T directly. So that's called an LSTM with peepholes. Another variation of the LSTM cell is called the gated recurrent unit or GRU. And this was actually introduced quite recently, around 2014. And what it ends up doing is it actually simplifies things a bit by combining the forget and input gates into a single, what they call, update gates. And it also merges the cell state and hidden state, and it makes a few other changes.

So this resulting model is actually simpler than standard LSTM models. And because of that, it's been growing increasingly popular over the last few years that it's been around. So there's actually lots of other slight variations off of this, and they're being introduced all the time. There was another one called depth gated recurrent neural networks, and that was introduced in 2015. And I mean, who knows, maybe in a few years, you'll see another variation that further improves on this model.

But the main idea is to understand how LSTMs work, and that allows you to quickly learn how these variations work off of it. All right, now that we understand LSTMs and GRUs, we can now use them with Keras. Now, fortunately, Keras has a super nice API that makes LSTM and RNN really easy to work with. And typically, from the research I've done, for text generation LSTMs work best. So that's what we're going to be using. Coming up next, we're gonna take a several part process in order to learn how to format our data, that is our text data, for recurrent neural networks.

So we'll be using the bAbI dataset or bAbI dataset released by Facebook research and there's a link there or you can just Google search the term in order to figure out more about the data sets.

We're using a particular subset of that dataset which has stories, questions, and answers. But there's actually lots of different versions with different types of questions. So the type of data we're gonna be working with has three main components. It has a story component. So for an example, we can have a simple story of Jane went to the store, Mike ran to the bedroom; And sometimes within the paper, they also refer to this as the sentences. So, we have a story and then we'll have a yes or no question.

And the question here is, is Mike in the store? And our network is going to be able to understand that since Mike ran to the bedroom, he is not in the store. So we have a correct answer of no. So again, three main components: the data, a story, a question or answer. So story or sentences, question or query and then an answer. So we're gonna be following along with a paper called End-to-End Memory Networks by the following authors. And a really key part to continuing on throughout these lectures is that you really must read the paper to understand how this network works.

We'll be doing a brief overview of the paper here but you should check out the resource link download the PDF and read the entire paper. It's gonna take a while to read. It's probably about an hour long to really read it and understand it but keep that in mind, that's gonna really help you understand how we're actually building the model and where we're getting our architecture of the network from. So the overall idea of the model is that it's going to take in a discrete set of inputs.

X one, X two, X et cetera... all the way to X of n and those will be the actual sentences or stories and those are gonna be stored in the memory. And then we'll also take a corresponding query Q and then we're gonna output an answer A So each of the X, Q, and A, so that's the story, the question, the answer are going to contain symbols coming from a dictionary with V amount of words. So we'll be calling V a vocabulary. So we'll have a set or dictionary that contains the entire vocabulary across the entire data sets.

Basically what words are there. Words like journeyed, bedroom, Sandra, Mike, the names, the places, how they got there; And we'll have a vocab length as well. So then the model is going to write all X to the memory up to a fixed buffer size and then finds a continuous representation for the X and Q. So let's actually explore what this would look like. And there's three main components to the End-to-End network. There's gonna be the input memory representation, basically how do we actually take in the stories and the questions.

Then we have an output memory representation and then we're gonna be able to generate a final prediction. And once we have that sub component of the network, we're essentially going to create a full model using recurrent neural networks with multiple layers. So let's first discuss what a single layer looks like. So this is a diagram representing the single layer. So this is a single layer case which implements a single memory hop operation. And then we'll show you how you can expand this to multiple hops memory simply using recurrent neural networks.

So we first start off with the first key component of the single layer which is the input memory representation. So what we're going to receive is an input set of X of one, X of two, X of three, et cetera. Remember, those are the sentences or stories to be stored in memory. And we're gonna convert that entire set of Xs into memory vectors. So those memory vectors, we're gonna call M sub I. So you can see here we essentially have two types of encoders, and the bottom one is the M sub I.

So that's one of the inputs there. So then what we're going to use is we'll use Keras for embedding this to convert sentences and then we later on have another embedding an encoder process for C sub I, and we'll also be getting that question or query calling a Q, and that's also going to be embedded. So we'll embed that to obtain an internal state which we will label U. So you can see at the bottom of the diagram we have question Q. It's going through an embedding process and then we have the result which is just gonna be an internal state of this embedding inside the single layer called U.

And in the embedding space. So within this single layer, we're going to compute the match between U and memory M sub I by taking the inner product followed by a softmax operation. So that will look something like this. We take that question, embed it through embedding B then we have U and then we're gonna take U transposed. Take the product of that with M sub I; Remember that's the embedding of the sentences and take the softmax of that. And then you get P sub I.

So you can essentially see that's what's going on from the bottom as well as the left hand side. So that's the input memory representation, and then we're going to need the output memory representation. So for the output memory representation, each X sub I has a corresponding output vector C sub I. And this is given in the simplest case by another embedding matrix, which we'll just call C. So that's kind of that top embedding, embedding C. Then the response vector from the memory O is then a sum over the transformed input C sub I weighted by the probability vector from the input.

And here's the equation: O is equal to the sum of P of I times C of I. And because the function from input to output is smooth, we can then compute gradients and back propagate through this. Then the final third step is generating a single final prediction. So in this single layer case what we're gonna do is the sum of the output vector O and the input embedding U is passed through a final weight matrix that we'll label a capital W here. And then a softmax is used to produce the predicted label.

So we have that final weight matrix W, and then we have O plus U, and we pass that in through a softmax. And that's essentially going to give us probabilities of the predicted answer. So note what's actually happening here. This network is actually going to produce a probability for every single word in the vocabulary. However, we should only expect some relatively high probability on either the yes or no and that'll become more clear when we actually code this out.

We can then expand this single layer into multiple layers using what we already know about recurrent neural networks. We're simply going to take the output of one of the single layers and have that be the input for the next layer. So you can see here that the logic is essentially the same as what we saw in the single layer. We're just repeating it over and over again. Taking the inputs from one layer and then setting that to be the output of the next layer just as we discussed previously with recurrent neural networks.

So let's begin now exploring how we can build out this network with Python and Keras. And please keep in mind, I would highly recommend that you read the paper before continuing on to the code.
